> **Deep analysis:** [D-09.2 Impact & Risk Assessments](../../DeepAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-09.2.md)

#### D-09.2 Impact & Risk Assessments

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

> **CRITICAL FOR D-09.2.** All 5 regulations overlap. Each imposes a distinct risk/impact assessment regime from a different angle: GDPR DPIA (Art. 35), NIS 2 risk analysis (Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 22 coordinated assessments), CRA cybersecurity risk assessment (Art. 13(3) + Annex VII §3), DORA ICT risk framework (Art. 6/7), AI Act FRIA (Art. 9). The assessments are NOT cross-referenced at the OJ level — each is a separate artefact with distinct triggers, content, and review cadences.

##### Participants

| Regulation | SO ID | 1-line summary | Scope/angle |
|---|---|---|---|
| GDPR | SO-GDPR-028 + SO-GDPR-036 | DPIA covering Art. 35(7) four content items (systematic description, necessity/proportionality, risks, mitigation); joint-controller arrangement (Art. 35, Art. 26). | Personal-data-rights impact; pre-launch for high-risk processing; reviewed on risk change. |
| NIS2 | SO-NIS2-022 + SO-NIS2-016 + SR-NIS2-028 (Art. 21(1)) | Policies on risk analysis + EU-level coordinated supply-chain risk assessments via Art. 22(1). | Entity-level operational risk analysis (Art. 21); EU-level coordinated assessments (Art. 22); no pre-launch DPIA. |
| CRA | SO-CRA-053 + SO-CRA-054 | Cybersecurity risk assessment documented + updated during support period; covers intended purpose + reasonably foreseeable use; maps Annex I Part I (2) applicability. | Product-level pre-market risk assessment; documented in Annex VII §3; reviewed on material change. |
| DORA | SO-DORA-017 (Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence) + SO-DORA-003 (Art. 7(2) continuous + yearly review) | ICT risk management framework with three-lines-of-defence + continuous ICT risk identification + yearly review of risk scenarios. | Financial-entity operational ICT risk framework; no pre-launch DPIA-equivalent. |
| AI_Act | SO-AIACT-001 | Continuous, iterative risk management system across AI lifecycle with 4-step process (identify/analyse known+foreseeable risks; estimate/evaluate misuse risks; evaluate post-market risks; adopt targeted measures). | AI-system pre-market + post-market risk management system; Art. 9 risk management as the FRIA-equivalent. |

##### Pairwise matrix

<!-- pair: GDPR,NIS2 -->
| GDPR ↔ NIS2 | **different-perspective** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-045, Art. 35(1)-(4); SR-GDPR-046, Art. 35(7); SR-GDPR-047, Art. 35(11)) | DPIA with four Art. 35(7) content items; reviewed on change of risk. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-045 / SR-GDPR-046 / SR-GDPR-047 |
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a); SR-NIS2-039, Art. 21(1) appropriate-and-proportionate measures; SR-NIS2-040, all-hazards methodology) | Documented risk-analysis methodology with proportional scope; no DPIA-like pre-launch obligation. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-035 / SR-NIS2-039 / SR-NIS2-040 |

**Why different-perspective**: GDPR DPIA is pre-launch for high-risk processing; NIS 2 risk analysis is operational (continuous, not pre-launch). The two regimes do not substitute — a controller-NSIE must operate both.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,CRA -->
| GDPR ↔ CRA | **different-perspective** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-045, Art. 35(1)-(4); SR-GDPR-046, Art. 35(7)) | DPIA with four Art. 35(7) content items. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-045 / SR-GDPR-046 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-064, Art. 13(2); SR-CRA-065, Art. 13(2) risk-assessment propagation across 6 phases; SR-CRA-066, Art. 13(3) documented + updated risk assessment; SR-CRA-079, Art. 13(3) documented/updated; SR-CRA-080, Annex VII §3 risk-assessment record; SR-CRA-081, systematic always-current record) | Cybersecurity risk assessment documented + updated during support period; Annex I Part I (2) applicability mapping. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-064 / SR-CRA-065 / SR-CRA-066 / SR-CRA-079 / SR-CRA-080 / SR-CRA-081 |

**Why different-perspective + CRITICAL TENSION**: GDPR DPIA assesses impact on data subjects' rights and freedoms; CRA cybersecurity risk assessment assesses cybersecurity risks to the product and its users. The two risk objects differ (rights-and-freedoms vs. cybersecurity). For a CRA-regulated product that processes personal data, both assessments are required — but the OJ does not require cross-referencing between the two. The CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 carve-out for AI Act Art. 9 unified-risk-assessment does not extend to GDPR DPIA.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,DORA -->
| GDPR ↔ DORA | **different-perspective** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-045, Art. 35(1)-(4)) | DPIA pre-launch. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-045 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence) | Three-lines-of-defence + asset identification/classification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-028 |

**Why different-perspective**: GDPR DPIA is pre-launch; DORA risk framework is operational continuous + yearly review. No pre-launch DPIA-equivalent in DORA.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: GDPR,AI_Act -->
| GDPR ↔ AI_Act | **different-perspective** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **GDPR** (SR-GDPR-045, Art. 35(1)-(4)) | DPIA pre-launch. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/GDPR/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-GDPR-045 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-001, Art. 9(1)+(2); SR-AIACT-002, Art. 9(2) 4-step list) | Continuous, iterative risk management system across AI lifecycle with 4-step process. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-001 / SR-AIACT-002 |

**Why different-perspective**: GDPR DPIA is pre-launch for high-risk processing; AI Act Art. 9 risk management is continuous across the AI lifecycle. The two assessments overlap in scope for AI systems processing personal data but the triggers differ (high-risk-processing trigger vs. high-risk-AI trigger). AI Act Recital 78 preserves GDPR; the two stack without substitution.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,CRA -->
| NIS2 ↔ CRA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a); SR-NIS2-025, Art. 21(3) sentence 2 take into account Art. 22 results) | Risk-analysis policy + take-into-account Art. 22 coordinated supply-chain assessments. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-035 / SR-NIS2-025 |
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-064, Art. 13(2); SR-CRA-066, Art. 13(3)) | Cybersecurity risk assessment documented + updated during support period. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-064 / SR-CRA-066 |

**Why complementary**: NIS 2 risk analysis is entity-level operational; CRA risk assessment is product-level pre-market. The two regimes meet at the supplier-customer interface — a CRA-conformant product's risk assessment is an input to the NIS 2 entity's supplier-risk register.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,DORA -->
| NIS2 ↔ DORA | **equal** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a)) | Risk-analysis policy. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-035 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence) | Three-lines-of-defence + asset identification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-028 |

**Why equal for financial entities**: Both regulations impose risk-analysis obligations. DORA's three-lines-of-defence is the dominant authority for financial entities under DORA's direct scope. NIS 2 applies to the financial entity only insofar as it is an NSIE outside DORA.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: NIS2,AI_Act -->
| NIS2 ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **NIS2** (SR-NIS2-035, Art. 21(2)(a)) | Risk-analysis policy. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/NIS2/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-NIS2-035 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-001, Art. 9(1); SR-AIACT-002, Art. 9(2)) | Continuous iterative risk management system with 4-step process. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-001 / SR-AIACT-002 |

**Why complementary**: NIS 2 risk analysis is entity-level operational; AI Act risk management is AI-system-level continuous. For NSIEs deploying high-risk AI, both apply — the AI Act layer adds the AI-specific 4-step process to the NIS 2 risk-analysis policy.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,DORA -->
| CRA ↔ DORA | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-064, Art. 13(2); SR-CRA-066, Art. 13(3)) | Cybersecurity risk assessment documented + updated. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-064 / SR-CRA-066 |
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4)) | Three-lines-of-defence + asset identification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-028 |

**Why complementary**: CRA risk assessment is product-level pre-market; DORA three-lines-of-defence is entity-level operational governance. The two regimes do not substitute — a DORA entity procuring CRA products must discharge both.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: CRA,AI_Act -->
| CRA ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **CRA** (SR-CRA-064, Art. 13(2); SR-CRA-066, Art. 13(3)) | Cybersecurity risk assessment documented + updated. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/CRA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-CRA-064 / SR-CRA-066 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-001, Art. 9(1); SR-AIACT-002, Art. 9(2)) | Continuous iterative risk management system. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-001 / SR-AIACT-002 |

**Why complementary + CRITICAL CONSOLIDATION RULE**: CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 explicitly permits the AI Act Art. 9 risk management system to substitute for the CRA cybersecurity risk assessment when both apply to the same product. See SR-CRA-066 ambiguity_notes for the cross-regulation consolidation. This is the only OJ-level cross-regulation consolidation rule in D-09.2.


<!-- /pair -->

<!-- pair: DORA,AI_Act -->
| DORA ↔ AI_Act | **complementary** | Tension/difference |
|---|---|---|
| **DORA** (SR-DORA-028, Art. 6(4)) | Three-lines-of-defence + asset identification. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/DORA/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-DORA-028 |
| **AI_Act** (SR-AIACT-001, Art. 9(1); SR-AIACT-002, Art. 9(2)) | Continuous iterative risk management system. | |
|   → full rationale: `../../../Regulation/AI_Act/02_SecurityRules_NIST.md` SR-AIACT-001 / SR-AIACT-002 |

**Why complementary**: DORA ICT risk framework is entity-level operational; AI Act risk management is AI-system-level continuous. The two stack without OJ-level consolidation (DORA has no equivalent to CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2).


<!-- /pair -->
##### Emergent tensions (5 regulations)
<!-- emergent: GDPR,NIS2,CRA,DORA,AI_Act -->

- **CRITICAL: Five overlapping risk/impact assessments with only one consolidation rule**: GDPR DPIA (Art. 35), NIS 2 risk analysis (Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 22), CRA cybersecurity risk assessment (Art. 13(3) + Annex VII §3), DORA ICT risk framework (Art. 6/7), AI Act FRIA-equivalent (Art. 9) — five separate assessment regimes that may all apply to the same entity-product combination. The ONLY OJ-level consolidation rule is CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 (AI Act Art. 9 risk management substitutes for CRA risk assessment). No other cross-regulation consolidation exists. Phase 1 entities must maintain five separate risk registers with no OJ-level cross-referencing. **Recommend Layer 1 cross-cutting analysis** to map the five assessment objects (data-subject rights, entity operational risk, product cybersecurity risk, ICT operational risk, AI-system risk) to a unified register.
- **Pre-launch vs. operational review asymmetry**: GDPR DPIA is pre-launch (reviewed on change of risk); NIS 2 risk analysis is continuous; CRA risk assessment is pre-market + support-period; DORA risk framework is continuous + yearly review; AI Act risk management is continuous iterative across lifecycle. The review cadences are not synchronised. An entity must satisfy five independent review triggers without a unified cadence.
- **GDPR DPIA does not address cybersecurity risk**: GDPR Art. 35(7) covers risks to data-subject rights and freedoms; it does not require cybersecurity-risk analysis. The CRA risk assessment covers cybersecurity risks; the NIS 2 / DORA risk frameworks cover operational ICT risks; the AI Act covers AI-system risks. None of the four non-GDPR assessments substitute for GDPR DPIA. For a CRA-regulated product processing personal data, the GDPR DPIA must be a separate artefact even when the CRA risk assessment has been performed.

<!-- /emergent -->

##### SR cross-validation
All 5 regulations map D-09.2 to the NIST CSF 2.0 Subcategory **ID.RA-05** (Threats, vulnerabilities, likelihoods, impacts used to understand inherent risk and inform risk response) as the convergent anchor. GDPR additionally maps **ID.RA-04** (Potential impacts and likelihoods of threats exploiting vulnerabilities are identified and recorded) for the DPIA-specific impact-and-likelihood dimension; AI Act additionally maps **GV.RM-04** (Strategic direction for risk response) for the continuous-iterative dimension; CRA additionally maps **ID.SC-04** (Suppliers prioritised by criticality) for the supply-chain risk dimension.

##### Downstream implication
D-09.2 is the most heavily-overlapped sub-domain. Phase 1 entities must implement five distinct assessment artefacts with no OJ-level consolidation. The Layer 1 cross-cutting analysis is required to map the five assessment objects. For DORA financial entities deploying high-risk AI products processing personal data, all five assessments apply simultaneously. The CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 AI-Act-substitution rule is the only OJ-level consolidation available.

> **Domain analysis:** [D-09.2 Impact & Risk Assessments](../../DomainAnalysis/D-09_Governance-Documentation/D-09.2.md)

#### D-09.2 Impact & Risk Assessments

<!-- participants: GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act -->

**Participants (from CRDA):** GDPR, NIS2, CRA, DORA, AI_Act

**Critical note (CRDA flag):** D-09.2 is the most heavily-overlapped sub-domain. All 5 regulations overlap with distinct risk/impact assessment regimes from different angles: GDPR DPIA (Art. 35), NIS 2 risk analysis (Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 22), CRA cybersecurity risk assessment (Art. 13(3) + Annex VII §3), DORA ICT risk framework (Art. 6/7), AI Act FRIA (Art. 9). The assessments are NOT cross-referenced at the OJ level — each is a separate artefact with distinct triggers, content, and review cadences. The ONLY OJ-level consolidation rule is CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 (AI Act Art. 9 risk management substitutes for CRA risk assessment).

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ NIS2

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 35(1) + Art. 35(7)):**
> "Where a type of processing is likely to result in a high risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, the controller shall, prior to processing, carry out an assessment of the impact of the envisaged processing operations on the protection of personal data …" — Art. 35(1) GDPR
> "The assessment shall contain at least: (a) a systematic description of the envisaged processing operations and the purposes of the processing, including, where applicable, the legitimate interest pursued by the controller; (b) an assessment of the necessity and proportionality of the processing operations in relation to the purposes; (c) an assessment of the risks to the rights and freedoms of data subjects; (d) the measures envisaged to address the risks …" — Art. 35(7) GDPR

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 21(1) + Art. 22(1)):**
> "[Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to … ] (a) policies on risk analysis and information system security …" — Art. 21(2)(a) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-035)
> "[Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate … measures to … ] taking into account the degree of exposure of the entity to risks … the likelihood of incidents … their severity …" — Art. 21(1) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-039)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: pre-launch DPIA for high-risk personal-data processing, with 4 Art. 35(7) content items.
- NIS 2 scope: operational (continuous) risk-analysis policy + methodology, not pre-launch.
- Scope overlap: **N (typically)** — different triggers (pre-launch DPIA vs. continuous operational analysis).

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: high-risk processing event.
- NIS 2 trigger: ongoing NIS operations + risk-analysis policy.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: DPIA before processing + review on change of risk (Art. 35(11)).
- NIS 2: continuous methodology.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: supervisory authority (DPA), data subjects.
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: 4-item Art. 35(7) content.
- NIS 2: not codified for content (entity-discretionary under Recital 56).

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR DPIA is pre-launch for high-risk processing; NIS 2 risk analysis is operational (continuous, not pre-launch). The two regimes do not substitute — a controller-NSIE must operate both. Different triggers and review cadences; no OJ-level consolidation rule.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Conditional (Y when the DPIA is conducted for an NSIE's processing; N otherwise)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 35 DPIA (pre-launch) + NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(a) continuous risk-analysis policy.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ CRA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 35(1) + Art. 35(7)):**
> [as quoted above]

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 13(2) + Art. 13(3) + Art. 13(4) sentence 2):**
> [as quoted above]
> "The cybersecurity risk assessment referred to in paragraph 2 shall be part of, or combined with, the risk assessment required by other Union legal acts applicable to the product with digital elements." — Art. 13(4) sentence 2 CRA

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: pre-launch DPIA for high-risk personal-data processing.
- CRA scope: pre-market + support-period cybersecurity risk assessment with 6-phase lifecycle propagation.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the CRA-regulated product processes personal data).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: high-risk processing event.
- CRA trigger: product placement on market + support-period maintenance.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT** (pre-launch for personal data vs. pre-market for product).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: DPIA before processing + review on change of risk.
- CRA: documented + updated during support period.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: DPA.
- CRA: MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: 4-item Art. 35(7) content.
- CRA: Annex VII §3 risk-assessment record + Annex I Part I (2) applicability mapping.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR DPIA assesses impact on data subjects' rights and freedoms; CRA cybersecurity risk assessment assesses cybersecurity risks to the product and its users. The two risk objects differ (rights-and-freedoms vs. cybersecurity). For a CRA-regulated product that processes personal data, both assessments are required — but the OJ does not require cross-referencing. The CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 carve-out for AI Act Art. 9 unified-risk-assessment does not extend to GDPR DPIA.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the CRA-regulated product processes personal data)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 35 DPIA + CRA Art. 13(3) cybersecurity risk assessment. No OJ-level consolidation rule for the GDPR/CRA pair.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ DORA

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 35(1) + Art. 35(7)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 6(4) + Art. 7(2)):**
> "Financial entities shall assign the responsibility for managing and overseeing ICT risk to a control function and ensure an appropriate level of independence … in accordance with the three lines of defence model, or an internal risk management and control model." — Art. 6(4) DORA (per SR-DORA-028)
> "Financial entities shall, on a continuous basis, identify all sources of ICT risk, in particular the risk exposure to and from other financial entities, and assess cyber threats and ICT vulnerabilities relevant to their ICT supported business functions, information assets and ICT assets." — Art. 7(2) sentence 1 DORA (per SR-DORA-004; the OJ-literal locus is Art. 8(2) per the v0.2 audit note)
> "Financial entities shall review on a regular basis, and at least yearly, the risk scenarios impacting them." — Art. 7(2) sentence 2 DORA (per SR-DORA-005; the OJ-literal locus is Art. 8(2) per the v0.2 audit note)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: pre-launch DPIA for high-risk personal-data processing.
- DORA scope: continuous ICT risk identification + at-least-yearly risk-scenario review + three-lines-of-defence.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is a controller processing personal data).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: high-risk processing event.
- DORA trigger: continuous ICT risk identification + yearly risk-scenario review.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: DPIA before processing + review on change of risk.
- DORA: continuous + at-least-yearly.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: DPA.
- DORA: NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: 4-item Art. 35(7) content.
- DORA: ICT risk identification register + risk-scenario register.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR DPIA is pre-launch; DORA risk framework is operational continuous + yearly review. No pre-launch DPIA-equivalent in DORA.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the DORA financial entity conducts a DPIA for high-risk personal-data processing)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 35 DPIA (pre-launch) + DORA Art. 7(2) continuous identification + Art. 7(2) sentence 2 yearly review.

---

##### Pair: GDPR ↔ AI_Act

**GDPR article (verbatim, Art. 35(1) + Art. 35(7)):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 9(1) + Art. 9(2)):**
> "A risk management system shall be established, implemented, documented and maintained in relation to high-risk AI systems." — Art. 9(1) AI Act (per SR-AIACT-001)
> "The risk management system shall comprise the following steps: (a) the identification and analysis of the known and the reasonably foreseeable risks to health, safety and fundamental rights; (b) the estimation and evaluation of the risks emerging from use under conditions of reasonably foreseeable misuse; (c) the evaluation of other risks arising from analysis of post-market monitoring data; (d) the adoption of appropriate and targeted risk management measures …" — Art. 9(2)(a)–(d) AI Act (per SR-AIACT-002)

**Scope comparison:**
- GDPR scope: pre-launch DPIA for high-risk personal-data processing.
- AI Act scope: continuous, iterative risk-management system across AI lifecycle with 4-step process.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when a high-risk AI system processes personal data).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- GDPR trigger: high-risk processing event.
- AI Act trigger: high-risk AI system lifecycle (continuous + iterative).
- Trigger alignment: **PARTIAL** (different cadences — pre-launch DPIA vs. continuous).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- GDPR: DPIA before processing + review on change of risk.
- AI Act: continuous + iterative across lifecycle.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- GDPR: DPA.
- AI Act: provider, MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- GDPR: 4-item Art. 35(7) content.
- AI Act: 4-step closed list (a)–(d) + Art. 27 FRIA for certain Annex III categories.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — DIFFERENT-PERSPECTIVE**
**Reasoning:** GDPR DPIA is pre-launch for high-risk processing; AI Act Art. 9 risk management is continuous across the AI lifecycle. The two assessments overlap in scope for AI systems processing personal data but the triggers differ (high-risk-processing trigger vs. high-risk-AI trigger). AI Act Recital 78 preserves GDPR; the two stack without substitution.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when a high-risk AI system processes personal data — Art. 27 FRIA may be required for certain Annex III categories)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: GDPR Art. 35 DPIA + AI Act Art. 9 risk-management system + Art. 27 FRIA where applicable. AI Act Recital 78 preservation is the operative OJ cross-link.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ CRA

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(a) + Art. 21(3) sentence 2 + Art. 22(1)):**
> "[Member States shall ensure that essential and important entities take appropriate and proportionate technical, operational and organisational measures to … ] (a) policies on risk analysis and information system security …" — Art. 21(2)(a) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-035)
> "When considering which of the measures referred to in paragraph 2, point (d), are appropriate, essential and important entities shall also take into account the results of the coordinated security risk assessments of critical supply chains carried out in accordance with Article 22(1) …" — Art. 21(3) sentence 2 NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-025)
> "The Cooperation Group may carry out coordinated security risk assessments of specific critical ICT services, ICT systems or ICT products supply chains …" — Art. 22(1) NIS 2 (per SR-NIS2-025)

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 13(2) + Art. 13(3)):**
> [as quoted above]

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS 2 scope: entity-level operational risk analysis + EU-level coordinated assessments (Art. 22).
- CRA scope: product-level pre-market risk assessment.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the entity procures CRA-conformant products — the CRA risk assessment is an input to the NIS 2 entity's supplier-risk register).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS 2 trigger: continuous operational analysis + EU-level coordinated assessments.
- CRA trigger: product placement on market + support-period maintenance.
- Trigger alignment: **DIFFERENT**.

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS 2: continuous + yearly review.
- CRA: documented + updated during support period.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority, Cooperation Group.
- CRA: MSA.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS 2: not codified for content (entity-discretionary under Recital 56).
- CRA: Annex VII §3 risk-assessment record.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** NIS 2 risk analysis is entity-level operational; CRA risk assessment is product-level pre-market. The two regimes meet at the supplier-customer interface — a CRA-conformant product's risk assessment is an input to the NIS 2 entity's supplier-risk register.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the entity procures CRA-conformant products)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: NIS 2 Art. 21(2)(a) risk-analysis policy + CRA Art. 13(3) product-level risk assessment as upstream input.

##### Pair: NIS2 ↔ DORA

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(a)):**
> [as quoted above]

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 6(4) + Art. 7(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

**Scope comparison:**
- NIS 2 scope: entity-level operational risk-analysis policy.
- DORA scope: financial-entity three-lines-of-defence + ICT risk identification + yearly risk-scenario review.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the DORA financial entity is also an NSIE).**

**Trigger comparison:**
- NIS 2 trigger: continuous operational analysis.
- DORA trigger: continuous ICT risk identification + yearly review.
- Trigger alignment: **SAME** (both operational continuous).

**Threshold/timeline comparison:**
- NIS 2: continuous.
- DORA: continuous + at-least-yearly.
- Tension: **NONE**.

**Recipient comparison:**
- NIS 2: management body, competent authority.
- DORA: NCA.

**Content template comparison:**
- NIS 2: not codified for content.
- DORA: risk identification register + risk-scenario register.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** EQUAL
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — EQUAL**
**Reasoning:** Both regulations impose risk-analysis obligations. DORA's three-lines-of-defence is the dominant authority for financial entities under DORA's direct scope. NIS 2 applies to the financial entity only insofar as it is an NSIE outside DORA.

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the DORA financial entity is also an NSIE applying risk-analysis)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce a DORA-dominant stack: DORA Art. 7(2) continuous identification + Art. 7(2) sentence 2 yearly review discharges both DORA's dedicated risk-analysis obligations and NIS 2's Art. 21(2)(a) policy artefacts for DORA financial entities.

**NIS2 article (verbatim, Art. 21(2)(a)):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 9(1) + Art. 9(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

##### Pair: CRA ↔ AI_Act

**CRA article (verbatim, Art. 13(4) sentence 2):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 9(1) + Art. 9(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

**Scope comparison:**
- CRA scope: product-level pre-market + support-period cybersecurity risk assessment.
- AI Act scope: AI-system continuous, iterative risk-management system with 4-step process.
- Scope overlap: **Y (when the CRA-regulated product is also a high-risk AI system).**

**Reasoning:** CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2 explicitly permits the AI Act Art. 9 risk management system to substitute for the CRA cybersecurity risk assessment when both apply to the same product. See SR-CRA-066 ambiguity_notes for the cross-regulation consolidation. **This is the only OJ-level cross-regulation consolidation rule in D-09.2.** No other cross-regulation consolidation exists.

##### Pair: DORA ↔ AI_Act

**DORA article (verbatim, Art. 6(4)):**
> [as quoted above]

**AI_Act article (verbatim, Art. 9(1) + Art. 9(2)):**
> [as quoted above]

**Scope comparison:**

- DORA: NCA.
- AI Act: MSA, AI Office.

**Content template comparison:**
- DORA: ICT risk identification register.
- AI Act: 4-step closed list.

**Classified relationship (from CRDA):** COMPLEMENTARY
**Verified relationship (after OJ-text analysis):** **SAME — COMPLEMENTARY**
**Reasoning:** DORA ICT risk framework is entity-level operational; AI Act risk management is AI-system-level continuous. The two stack without OJ-level consolidation (DORA has no equivalent to CRA Art. 13(4) sentence 2).

**Scope-disjoint test:** Does this pair actually overlap on a single event at a single party? **Y (when the DORA financial entity deploys high-risk AI)**. No reclassification required.

**Downstream implication (Phase 1C Doc 07):** Phase 2 obligation derivation must produce two separate artefacts: DORA Art. 6(4) three-lines-of-defence + AI Act Art. 9 risk-management system. No OJ-level consolidation.

# Part 4 — Relevant Ambiguity (by regulation)

## GDPR

##### Art. 9(2)(g) — Substantial public interest

**Clause: GDPR-CL23** | type: lawfulness base (special category) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-MEMBER-STATE-LAW

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`substantial public interest`, `proportionate`, `essence of the right to data protection`, `suitable and specific measures`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `substantial public interest`**

`substantial public interest` inherits the Art. 6(1)(e) `public interest` ambiguity plus the `substantial` qualifier. The clause cross-references Charter Art. 52(1) via `essence of the right`.

S3 — same family of ambiguity as Art. 6(1)(e), but more severe because `substantial` is undefined.

---

##### Art. 9(2)(j) — Research/statistics/archiving (with Art. 89(1) safeguards)

**Clause: GDPR-CL24** | type: lawfulness base (special category, research) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-MEMBER-STATE-LAW

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`archiving purposes in the public interest`, `proportionate`, `essence`).

The clause cross-references Art. 89(1) for the substantive safeguards (pseudonymisation, no decisions about data subjects, etc.). The Art. 89(1) reference provides partial anchoring but does not define `archiving`.

---

### Article 10 — Criminal convictions and offences

#### Verbatim

> **Article 10**
>
> Processing of personal data relating to criminal convictions and offences shall be carried out only under the control of official authority or when the processing is authorised by Union or Member State law providing for appropriate safeguards for the rights and freedoms of data subjects. Any **comprehensive register** of criminal convictions shall be kept only under the control of official authority.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 13(2)(f) — Automated decision-making information

**Clause: GDPR-RT06** | type: information duty (ADM-specific) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-COLLECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`meaningful information`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `meaningful information about the logic involved, as well as the significance and the envisaged consequences`**

`meaningful information` + `the logic involved` are partially self-contradictory: full logic disclosure would expose trade secrets; meaningful-but-not-full disclosure has no threshold. This is the central automated-decision-making transparency question (cf. AI Act Art. 13, Art. 14 cross-over).

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `meaningful` = accurate, intelligible, sufficient for the data subject to challenge. | EDPB Guidelines on ADM 2018. |
| R2 | `meaningful` = a description of categories of decisions and main criteria. | Industry reading; partial disclosure. |
| R3 | `meaningful` = the full logic including weights and thresholds. | Strict pro-data-subject reading. |

R1 vs R3 yield materially distinct explainability engineering scope.

---

### Article 14 — Information when not collected from data subject

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 14**
> **Information to be provided where personal data have not been obtained from the data subject**
>
> 1. Where personal data have not been obtained from the data subject, the controller shall provide the data subject with the following information:

> (a) the identity and the contact details of the controller and, where applicable, of the controller's representative;

> (b) the contact details of the data protection officer, where applicable;

> (c) the purposes of the processing for which the personal data are intended as well as the legal basis for the processing;

> (d) the categories of personal data concerned;

> (e) the recipients or categories of recipients of the personal data, if any;

> (f) where applicable, that the controller intends to transfer personal data to a recipient in a third country or international organisation and the existence or absence of an adequacy decision by the Commission (...)

> 2. In addition to the information referred to in paragraph 1, the controller shall provide the data subject with the following further information necessary to ensure fair and transparent processing in respect of the data subject:

> (a) the period for which the personal data will be stored, or if that is not possible, the criteria used to determine that period;

> (b) where the processing is based on point (f) of Article 6(1), the legitimate interests pursued by the controller or by a third party;

> (c) the existence of the right to request from the controller access to and rectification or erasure of personal data or restriction of processing concerning the data subject and to object to processing as well as the right to data portability;

> (d) where processing is based on point (a) of Article 6(1) or point (a) of Article 9(2), the existence of the right to withdraw consent at any time (...);

> (e) the right to lodge a complaint with a supervisory authority;

> (f) from which source the personal data originate, and if applicable, whether it came from publicly accessible sources;

> (g) the existence of automated decision-making, including profiling, referred to in Article 22(1) and (4) and, at least in those cases, meaningful information about the logic involved, as well as the significance and the envisaged consequences of such processing for the data subject.

> 3. The controller shall provide the information referred to in paragraphs 1 and 2:

> (a) within a reasonable period after obtaining the personal data, but at the latest within one month, having regard to the specific circumstances in which the personal data are processed;

> (b) if the personal data are to be used for communication with the data subject, at the latest at the time of the first communication to that data subject; or

> (c) if a disclosure to another recipient is envisaged, at the latest when the personal data are first disclosed.

> 4. Where the controller intends to further process the personal data for a purpose other than that for which the personal data were obtained, the controller shall provide the data subject prior to that further processing with information on that other purpose (...)

> 5. Paragraphs 1 to 4 shall not apply where and insofar as:

> (a) the data subject already has the information;

> (b) the provision of such information proves impossible or would involve a disproportionate effort, in particular for processing for archiving purposes in the public interest, scientific or historical research purposes or statistical purposes, subject to the conditions and safeguards referred to in Article 89(1) (...);

> (c) obtaining or disclosure is expressly laid down by Union or Member State law (...); or

> (d) where the personal data must remain confidential subject to an obligation of professional secrecy (...).

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 21(1) — Objection grounds and overriding

**Clause: GDPR-RT16** | type: data subject right (object) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-OBJECTION

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`compelling legitimate grounds`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `compelling legitimate grounds`**

`compelling` is stronger than Art. 6(1)(f) `legitimate interests`. The `overriding` test for compelling grounds is undefined.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Compelling` = strictly necessary (lex specialis to legitimate interests). | EDPB Guidelines on legitimate interests. |
| R2 | `Compelling` = materially more weighty than legitimate interests. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct balance-tests in objection handling.

---

##### Art. 21(3) — Absolute bar on direct-marketing after objection

**Clause: GDPR-RT17** | type: data subject right (absolute) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-OBJECTION

**Berry anchor:** none — Art. 21(3) is a clear absolute obligation.

**Instance 1 — none** — the clause is unambiguous: once the data subject objects to direct-marketing processing, the processing must cease. No variant readings.

---

### Article 22 — Automated individual decision-making

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 22**
> **Automated individual decision-making, including profiling**
>
> 1. The data subject shall have the right not to be subject to a decision based solely on automated processing, including profiling, which produces legal effects concerning him or her or similarly significantly affects him or her.

> 2. Paragraph 1 shall not apply if the decision:

> (a) is necessary for entering into, or performance of, a contract between the data subject and a data controller;

> (b) is authorised by Union or Member State law to which the controller is subject and which also lays down suitable measures to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests; or

> (c) is based on the data subject's explicit consent.

> 3. In the cases referred to in points (a) and (c) of paragraph 2, the data controller shall implement **suitable measures** to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests, at least the right to obtain **human intervention** on the part of the controller, to express his or her point of view and to contest the decision.

> 4. Decisions referred to in paragraph 2 shall not be based on special categories of personal data referred to in Article 9(1), unless point (a) or (g) of Article 9(2) applies and suitable measures to safeguard the data subject's rights and freedoms and legitimate interests are in place.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 22(1) — General prohibition on solely-automated decisions

**Clause: GDPR-RT18** | type: data subject right (ADM protection) | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-DECISION

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`solely`, `significantly`).

**Instance 1 — POLY / S3 / `solely`, `similarly significantly affects`**

`Solely` excludes hybrid decisions (human review with AI recommendation) but the boundary (when does human review break the `solely` qualifier?) is undefined. `Similarly significantly affects` is undefined — what level of significance crosses the threshold?

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Solely` = no human involvement in the decision-making chain. | Strict text. |
| R2 | `Solely` = human involvement is rubber-stamping, not substantive review. | EDPB Guidelines on ADM. |
| R3 | `Solely` = human can override but AI makes the default decision. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R3 yield materially distinct HITL (human-in-the-loop) engineering requirements.

**Variant readings for `significantly`:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Significant = financial impact, access to services, reputation. | EDPB Guidelines on ADM 2018. |
| R2 | Significant = any non-trivial impact on the data subject's life. | Strict pro-data-subject reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct decision-classification systems.

---

##### Art. 26(1) — Joint-controller determination

**Clause: GDPR-CP04** | type: multi-controller | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER (joint) | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`jointly determine` — see Art. 4(7) ambiguity), §5.1 (`transparent`).

**Instance 1 — POLY / S3 / `jointly determine`**

Inherits Art. 4(7) `alone or jointly with others` ambiguity. The CJEU IAB Europe case (C-604/22, 2024) held that joint controllership can exist even when one party lacks direct influence on processing purposes — extending the scope of `jointly determine` beyond the original EDPB reading.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Jointly determine` = shared decision-making authority on purposes. | EDPB Guidelines 07/2020 (original). |
| R2 | `Jointly determine` = either party having determinative influence on purposes. | CJEU IAB Europe (C-604/22). |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct joint-controller assignment.

---

##### Art. 26(2) — Joint-controller arrangement essentials

**Clause: GDPR-CP05** | type: multi-controller transparency | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER (joint) | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`duly reflect`, `essence`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S2 / `duly reflect`**

`duly` is the recurring vague adverb. S2 because the substantive obligation (reflect roles in arrangement) is unambiguous.

### Article 27 — Representatives of non-EU controllers/processors

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 27**
> **Representatives of controllers or processors not established in the Union**
>
> 1. Where Article 3(2) applies, the controller or the processor shall designate **in writing** a **representative** in the Union.

> 2. The obligation laid down in paragraph 1 of this Article shall not apply to:

> (a) processing which is **occasional**, does not include, on a large scale, processing of special categories of data as referred to in Article 9(1) or processing of personal data relating to criminal convictions and offences referred to in Article 10, and is unlikely to result in a risk to the rights and freedoms of natural persons, taking into account the nature, context, scope and purposes of the processing; or

> (b) a public authority or body.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 32(1) — Security measures (open list)

**Clause: GDPR-CP15** | type: security | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`state of the art`, `appropriate`, `as appropriate`), §5.4.7 (4-element list), §3.3.1 (CIA+R polysemy).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `state of the art`**

Cross-clause propagation — same as Art. 25(1). S3 because security-engineering scope is material to compliance.

**Instance 2 — COORD / S3 / `(a) AND (b) AND (c) AND (d)`**

The 4-element list `(a) pseudonymisation/encryption, (b) CIA+R, (c) restore-in-timely-manner, (d) regular-testing` is closed (and). All 4 required.

**Instance 3 — POLY+VAG / S3 / `in a timely manner` (Art. 32(1)(c))**

`Timely manner` is the textbook Berry-flagged temporal vague phrase. Recital 49 (non-binding) attempts to fix: "restore the availability and access as soon as possible". The cross-reference is non-binding and the operational meaning drifts.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Timely` = RTO defined per processing activity (objective measure). | Industry standard (BCP/DRP best practice). |
| R2 | `Timely` = as-soon-as-practicable for the specific incident. | EDPB Guidelines 7/2019 (generic). |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct BCP/DRP engineering scope.

---

##### Art. 35(1) — DPIA trigger

**Clause: GDPR-CP21** | type: impact assessment | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-PROCESSING (pre-launch)

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`new technologies`, `likely`, `high risk`), §3.3.1 (`new` polysemy).

**Instance 1 — POLY+VAG / S3 / `new technologies` + `high risk`**

`New technologies` is the recurring time-dependent vague phrase (cf. Art. 25 `state of the art`). Was cloud `new` in 2018? Is generative AI `new` in 2026? EDPB Guidelines 4/2019 attempt to fix but `new` is inherently time-relative.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `New technologies` = technologies not widely deployed in the industry at time of assessment. | EDPB Guidelines 4/2019. |
| R2 | `New technologies` = listed in supervisory-authority Art. 35(4) lists. | EDPB reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct DPIA-trigger classification.

---

##### Art. 35(7) — DPIA content

**Clause: GDPR-CP22** | type: impact assessment content | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-DPIA

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`systematic description`, `necessity and proportionality`).

**Instance 1 — POLY / S2 / `necessity and proportionality`**

Three different `necessity` senses across GDPR (5(1)(c) data-minimisation, 6(1)(b) contract, 35(7)(b) DPIA) — same term, different operationalisation. S2 because Art. 35(7)(b) is internally anchored.

### Article 36 — Prior consultation

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 36**
> **Prior consultation**
>
> 1. The controller shall consult the supervisory authority **prior to processing** where a data protection impact assessment under Article 35 indicates that the processing would result in a **high risk** in the absence of measures taken by the controller to mitigate the risk.

> 2. Where the supervisory authority is of the opinion that the intended processing referred to in paragraph 1 would infringe this Regulation, in particular where the controller has insufficiently identified or mitigated the risk, the supervisory authority shall, **within period of up to eight weeks** of receipt of the request for consultation, provide written advice to the controller and, where applicable to the processor (...).

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 36(1) — Prior consultation trigger

**Clause: GDPR-CP23** | type: supervisory consultation | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER | obligationType: PER-PROCESSING (pre-launch)

**Berry anchor:** §3.3.1 (`high risk` — Art. 35 inheritance).

**Instance 1 — POLY+VAG / S2 / `high risk in the absence of measures`**

Inherits Art. 35(1) `high risk` threshold. S2 because the trigger is the same DPIA outcome.

---

##### Art. 37(1)(b) — DPO designation (monitoring threshold)

**Clause: GDPR-CP25** | type: DPO designation | obligatedParty: CONTROLLER + PROCESSOR | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`core activities`, `regular and systematic`, `large scale`).

**Instance 1 — VAG / S3 / `core activities`**

`Core activities` is the Berry-flagged term. EDPB Guidelines 3/2018 attempt to fix as primary business activities (vs ancillary/supporting).

**Instance 2 — VAG+POLY / S3 / `regular and systematic`, `large scale`, `nature, scope and/or purposes`**

Three qualifiers combined with AND (`regular AND systematic AND large scale`) for monitoring-threshold determination. The `and/or` in the secondary clause (nature/scope/purposes) is the Berry §5.1 explicitly-flagged `and/or` problem.

**Variant readings:**
| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | `Core activities` = main revenue-driving business activities. | EDPB Guidelines 3/2018. |
| R2 | `Core activities` = activities that are essential to achieving the controller's purpose. | Loose reading. |

R1 vs R2 yield materially distinct DPO-designation policies.

---

### Article 38 — Position of the DPO

#### Verbatim (with highlighting)

> **Article 38**
> **Position of the data protection officer**
>
> 1. The controller and the processor shall ensure that the data protection officer is involved, **properly and in a timely manner**, in all issues which relate to the protection of personal data.

> 2. The controller and processor shall support the data protection officer in performing the tasks referred to in Article 39 by providing **resources necessary** to carry out those tasks and access to personal data and processing operations, and to maintain his or her expert knowledge.

> 3. The controller and processor shall ensure that the data protection officer does not receive any instructions regarding the exercise of those tasks. He or she shall not be dismissed or penalised by the controller or the processor for performing his tasks. The data protection officer shall **directly report to the highest management level** of the controller or the processor.

> 4. Data subjects may contact the data protection officer with regard to all issues related to processing of their personal data and to the exercise of their rights under this Regulation.

> 5. The data protection officer shall be bound by **secrecy or confidentiality** concerning the performance of his or her tasks, in accordance with Union or Member State law.

> 6. The data protection officer may fulfil other tasks and duties. The controller or processor shall ensure that any such tasks and duties do not result in a **conflict of interests**.

#### Clause breakdown

##### Art. 39(1)(b) — DPO monitoring and training

**Clause: GDPR-CP28** | type: DPO tasks | obligatedParty: DPO | obligationType: CONTINUOUS

**Berry anchor:** §5.1 (`awareness-raising`, `training`).

**Instance 1 — POLY+VAG / S2 / `awareness-raising and training`**

Inherits the GDPR/T1 `training` polysemy. S2 because the same training-practice applies.

### Chapter IV synthesis

#### Counts

| Category | Instances | Clauses affected |
|---|---|---|
| VAG | 32 | 28 / 28 |
| POLY | 16 | 16 / 28 |
| COORD | 19 | 18 / 28 |
| SCOPE-Q | 8 | 8 / 28 |
| **Total** | **75** | **28 / 28** |

| Severity | Instances |
|---|---|
| S1 — Low | 0 |
| S2 — Medium | 50 |
| S3 — High | 25 |

#### Recurring patterns

- `appropriate` appears in 11 of 28 clauses — cross-clause Berry-flagged term.
- `state of the art` appears in 2 clauses (Art. 25(1), Art. 32(1)) — propagates from Art. 5(1)(f) `appropriate security`.
- `high risk` appears in 4 clauses (Art. 34, 35, 36, 33 with different threshold) — three-threshold polysemy.
- `and/or` appears in 1 clause (Art. 37(1)(b)) — Berry §5.1 explicitly flagged.

#### Cross-clause structural observations

1. **Art. 24–28 (responsibility allocation)** — open by design. Each article cross-references Art. 5(1) principles and Art. 6 lawfulness bases. Controller/processor identification (Art. 4(7)/(8)) is the prerequisite.
2. **Art. 30–32 (documentation and security)** — the operational backbone. Art. 30 records + Art. 32 security measures + Art. 33/34 breach notifications form the compliance evidence chain.
3. **Art. 33–34 (breach notification)** — three-tier threshold architecture (`risk` Art. 33, `high risk` Art. 34, `high risk` Art. 35). EDPB Guidelines 9/2022 attempt to fix.
4. **Art. 35–36 (DPIA + consultation)** — pre-launch governance. Mandatory for high-risk processing (Art. 35(3) list).
5. **Art. 37–39 (DPO regime)** — independent oversight mechanism. Three designation triggers (Art. 37(1)(a)/(b)/(c)). EDPB Guidelines 3/2018 set operational threshold.

#### Anti-pattern notes for downstream files

- Chapter V inherits Art. 28 processor obligations (transfer is processing via a processor). Art. 28(3) DPA clauses must include transfer safeguards.

## CRA

#### 3.1 CRA-CL01 — Art. 6 chapeau + (a) — Essential-requirements gate (Part I)

**Source locus:**

> Products with digital elements shall be made available on the market only where:
> **(a)** they meet the **<mark>essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I</mark>** …;

**Instance 1 — VAG — S2 — `essential cybersecurity requirements`**

Inherits from Annex I Part I (analysed in `09_CRA_AnnexI_EssentialRequirements.md`). The `essential` modifier is itself undefined.

- **R1:** `essential` = mandatory (all Part I requirements must be met).
- **R2:** `essential` = core (some Part I requirements apply, depending on risk assessment).
- **R3:** `essential` = minimum baseline (additional requirements allowed).

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | All | In-Directive: Art. 13(3) `shall indicate whether and, if so in what manner, the security requirements set out in Part I, point (2), of Annex I are applicable` |
| R2 | Conditional | In-Directive: Art. 13(3) implies Part I (2) is conditional on risk assessment |
| R3 | Baseline | None in OJ |

R2 is the literal reading (Part I (1) is mandatory; Part I (2) is conditional). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`essential`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `meet`**

`meet` — what threshold?

- **R1:** Pass conformity assessment (Art. 32).
- **R2:** Self-declare conformity.
- **R3:** Demonstrate by technical documentation.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Third-party verified | None in OJ; Art. 32(1)(a) module A (internal) |
| R2 | Self-declared | In-Directive: Art. 32(1)(a) internal control |
| R3 | Documented | In-Directive: Art. 13(12) technical documentation |

R1/R2/R3 are class-dependent (Art. 32 routes products to modules). S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`meet`).

**Instance 3 — VAG — S2 — `shall be made available on the market only where`**

The "only where" is a **necessary condition** reading. The reading:

- **R1:** Strict necessary condition (no market access without Annex I conformity).
- **R2:** Necessary condition with carve-outs (e.g. trade fairs per Art. 4(2)).
- **R3:** Necessary condition with delegation to implementing acts.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Strict | In-Directive: literal `only where` |
| R2 | With Art. 4(2) carve-outs | In-Directive: Art. 4(2) trade-fair presentation |
| R3 | With IA carve-outs | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading; R2 is the harmonised reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`only where`).

---

## CRA v0.2 — Article 13 Obligations of Manufacturers (THE HEART)

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 34–37. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~2177–2370.
>
> **Pattern.** Article quoted in full (paragraph by paragraph), then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention applied.
>
> **Article 13 is the operational core of CRA** — 25 paragraphs, ~35 atomic clauses. It is the analog of GDPR Art. 32 and NIS 2 Art. 21. It defines the 6-phase product-lifecycle obligations (planning, design, development, production, delivery, maintenance), the cybersecurity risk assessment, the due-diligence duty on third-party components, the support period, the technical documentation, and the corrective-measures duty.

---

### 1. Article 13 — full text (key paragraphs)

> **Article 13 — Obligations of manufacturers**
>
> **1.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been designed, developed and produced in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

> **2.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall undertake an assessment of the cybersecurity risks associated with a product with digital elements and take the outcome of that assessment into account during the **planning, design, development, production, delivery and maintenance phases** of the product with digital elements with a view to minimising cybersecurity risks, preventing incidents and minimising their impact, **including in relation to the health and safety of users**.

> **3.** The cybersecurity risk assessment shall be documented and updated as appropriate during a support period to be determined in accordance with paragraph 8 of this Article. That cybersecurity risk assessment shall comprise at least an analysis of cybersecurity risks based on the **intended purpose and reasonably foreseeable use, as well as the conditions of use, of the product with digital elements**, such as the operational environment or the assets to be protected, taking into account the length of time the product is expected to be in use. The cybersecurity risk assessment shall indicate whether and, if so in what manner, the security requirements set out in Part I, point (2), of Annex I are applicable to the relevant product with digital elements and how those requirements are implemented as informed by the cybersecurity risk assessment. It shall also indicate how the manufacturer is to apply Part I, point (1), of Annex I and the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.

> **4.** When placing a product with digital elements on the market, the manufacturer shall include the cybersecurity risk assessment referred to in paragraph 3 of this Article in the technical documentation required pursuant to Article 31 and Annex VII. …

> **5.** For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall **exercise due diligence when integrating components sourced from third parties** so that those components do not compromise the cybersecurity of the product with digital elements, including when integrating components of free and open-source software that have not been made available on the market in the course of a commercial activity.

> **6.** Manufacturers shall, upon identifying a vulnerability in a component, including in an open source-component, which is integrated in the product with digital elements report the vulnerability to the person or entity manufacturing or maintaining the component, and **address and remediate** the vulnerability in accordance with the vulnerability handling requirements set out in Part II of Annex I. …

> **7.** The manufacturers shall systematically document, in a manner that is proportionate to the nature and the cybersecurity risks, **relevant cybersecurity aspects** concerning the products with digital elements, including vulnerabilities of which they become aware and any relevant information provided by third parties, and shall, where applicable, update the cybersecurity risk assessment of the products.

> **8.** Manufacturers shall ensure, when placing a product with digital elements on the market, and for the support period, that vulnerabilities of that product, including its components, are handled effectively and in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part II of Annex I.
>
> Manufacturers shall determine the support period so that it reflects the length of time during which the product is expected to be in use, taking into account, in particular, **reasonable user expectations, the nature of the product, including its intended purpose**, as well as **relevant Union law determining the lifetime** of products with digital elements. When determining the support period, manufacturers may also take into account the support periods of products with digital elements offering a similar functionality placed on the market by other manufacturers, the availability of the operating environment, the support periods of integrated components that provide core functions and are sourced from third parties as well as relevant guidance provided by the dedicated administrative cooperation group (ADCO) established pursuant to Article 52(15) and the Commission. **The matters to be taken into account in order to determine the support period shall be considered in a manner that ensures proportionality.**
>
> Without prejudice to the second subparagraph, the support period shall be **at least five years**. Where the product with digital elements is expected to be in use for less than five years, the support period shall correspond to the expected use time.
>
> …
>
> **9.** Manufacturers shall ensure that each security update, as referred to in Part II, point (8), of Annex I, which has been made available to users during the support period, **remains available after it has been issued for a minimum of 10 years or for the remainder of the support period, whichever is longer**.

> **10.** Where a manufacturer has placed subsequent substantially modified versions of a software product on the market, that manufacturer may ensure compliance with the essential cybersecurity requirement set out in Part II, point (2), of Annex I only for the version that it has last placed on the market, provided that theusers of the versions that were previously placed on the market have access to the version last placed on the market free of charge and do not incur additional costs to adjust the hardware and software environment in which they use the original version of that product.

> **11.** Manufacturers may maintain public software archives enhancing user access to historical versions. In those cases, users shall be clearly informed in an easily accessible manner about risks associated with using unsupported software.

> **12.** Before placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall draw up the technical documentation referred to in Article 31. They shall carry out the chosen conformity assessment procedures as referred to in Article 32 or have them carried out. Where compliance … has been demonstrated by that conformity assessment procedure, manufacturers shall draw up the EU declaration of conformity in accordance with Article 28 and affix the CE marking in accordance with Article 30.

> **13.** Manufacturers shall **keep the technical documentation and the EU declaration of conformity at the disposal of the market surveillance authorities for at least 10 years** after the product with digital elements has been placed on the market or for the support period, whichever is longer.

> **14.** Manufacturers shall ensure that procedures are in place for products with digital elements that are part of a series of production to remain in conformity with this Regulation. …

> **15.** Manufacturers shall ensure that their products with digital elements bear a **type, batch or serial number or other element allowing their identification** …

> **16.** Manufacturers shall indicate the **name, registered trade name or registered trademark of the manufacturer, and the postal address, email address or other digital contact details**, as well as, where applicable, the website where the manufacturer can be contacted, on the product with digital elements …

> **17.** For the purposes of this Regulation, manufacturers shall designate a **single point of contact** to enable users to communicate directly and rapidly with them, including in order to facilitate reporting on vulnerabilities of the product with digital elements. …

> **18.** Manufacturers shall ensure that products with digital elements are accompanied by the **information and instructions to the user set out in Annex II**, in paper or electronic form. …

> **19.** Manufacturers shall ensure that the **end date of the support period** referred to in paragraph 8, including at least the month and the year, is clearly and understandably specified at the time of purchase …

> **20.** Manufacturers shall either provide a copy of the EU declaration of conformity or a **simplified EU declaration of conformity** with the product with digital elements. …

> **21.** From the placing on the market and for the support period, manufacturers who know or have reason to believe that the product with digital elements or the processes put in place by the manufacturer are not in conformity with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Annex I shall **immediately take the corrective measures** necessary to bring that product with digital elements or the manufacturer's processes into conformity, or to withdraw or recall the product, as appropriate.

> **22.** Manufacturers shall, upon a reasoned request from a market surveillance authority, provide that authority, in a language which can be easily understood by that authority, with all the information and documentation, in paper or electronic form, necessary to demonstrate the conformity …

> **23.** A manufacturer that ceases its operations and, as a result, is not able to comply with this Regulation shall inform, before the cessation of operations takes effect, the relevant market surveillance authorities as well as, by any means available and to the extent possible, the users of the relevant products with digital elements placed on the market, of the impending cessation of operations.

> **24.** The Commission may, by means of implementing acts taking into account European or international standards and best practices, specify the format and elements of the **software bill of materials** referred to in Part II, point (1), of Annex I. …

> **25.** In order to assess the dependence of Member States and of the Union as a whole on software components and in particular on components qualifying as free and open-source software, ADCO may decide to conduct a Union wide **dependency assessment** for specific categories of products with digital elements. …

---

**Total: 36 atomic clauses for Art. 13 alone** (35 if CRA-CL23b is merged into CL23a; the v0.2 split per Q6=b places CRA-C24a on Art. 13(5)).

**Note on Q6=b (CRA-C24 split):** T2 CRA-C24 was mapped to Art. 13(5) but described the 10-year documentation retention (which is actually Art. 13(13)). Per Q6=b, v0.2 splits this:
- CRA-CL23a (this file) = Art. 13(5) **due-diligence on third-party components** (the OJ-literal mapping).
- CRA-CL38 (this file) = Art. 13(13) **10-year documentation retention** (the substance T2 misattributed).

### 3. Detailed cards

(Selected highlights — full analysis is below.)

#### 3.1 CRA-CL14 — Art. 13(1) — Design/development/production duty

**Source locus:**

> When placing a product with digital elements on the market, manufacturers shall ensure that it has been **designed, developed and produced** in accordance with the essential cybersecurity requirements set out in Part I of Annex I.

**Instance 1 — COORD — S2 — `designed AND developed AND produced`**

The 3-phase coordination. Three readings:
- **R1:** Three distinct phases (each must comply).
- **R2:** Composite description (one process).
- **R3:** Each independently required.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distinct | In-Directive: literal `designed, developed and produced` |
| R2 | Composite | None in OJ |
| R3 | Independent | None in OJ |

R1 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (three-way coordination).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `placing on the market`**

Inherits Art. 3(21) definition: `first making available of a product with digital elements on the Union market`. Same POLY as Art. 3(21) D21.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`placing on the market`).

---

#### 3.2 CRA-CL15 — Art. 13(2) sentence 1 — Cybersecurity risk-assessment core duty

**Source locus:**

> For the purpose of complying with paragraph 1, manufacturers shall undertake an **assessment of the cybersecurity risks** associated with a product with digital elements …

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `cybersecurity risks`**

Cross-references Art. 3(37) `cybersecurity risk`. The term's ambiguity propagates here.

- **R1:** Risks to the product (incoming threats).
- **R2:** Risks from the product (outgoing to users).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Incoming | None in OJ |
| R2 | Outgoing | In-Directive: Art. 13(2) `impact … including in relation to the health and safety of users` |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `associated with a product with digital elements` |

R3 is the literal reading. S3.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`cybersecurity risks`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `assessment`**

`assessment` — methodology?

- **R1:** Quantitative (FAIR, NIST 800-30).
- **R2:** Qualitative (heat-map).
- **R3:** Either.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Quantitative | None in OJ |
| R2 | Qualitative | None in OJ |
| R3 | Either | In-Directive: literal `assessment` (no methodology specified) |

R3 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`assessment`).

---

#### 3.3 CRA-CL16 — Art. 13(2) sentence 1 — 6-phase lifecycle propagation

**Source locus:**

> …and take the outcome of that assessment into account during the **planning, design, development, production, delivery and maintenance phases** of the product with digital elements …

**Instance 1 — COORD — S3 — 6-phase coordination**

The 6-phase chain is the **longest single coordination pattern in CRA**. Six distinct lifecycle phases coordinated by AND. Three readings:
- **R1:** All six required.
- **R2:** Phases are illustrative (entity may organise differently).
- **R3:** The risk assessment applies to each phase but the obligation is to "take into account" the outcome.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | All six | In-Directive: literal `during the planning, design, development, production, delivery and maintenance phases` |
| R2 | Illustrative | None in OJ |
| R3 | Take-into-account | In-Directive: literal `take the outcome of that assessment into account` |

R1 is the literal reading. R3 admits partial compliance (some phases may not require full outcome-integration). R1 vs R3 produce materially distinct compliance populations. S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.5 (six-way coordination); §5.1 (`take into account`).

**Instance 2 — POLY — S2 — `delivery`**

`delivery` — to distributor? to end-user? to installer?

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Distributor | None in OJ |
| R2 | End-user | None in OJ |
| R3 | Installer | None in OJ |
| R4 | Any | In-Directive: literal `delivery` |

R4 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`delivery`).

**Instance 3 — POLY — S2 — `maintenance`**

`maintenance` — post-market only? Includes security updates?

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Post-market only | None in OJ |
| R2 | Includes security updates | In-Directive: Art. 13(9) security updates |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `maintenance` |

R3 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`maintenance`).

---

#### 3.4 CRA-CL17 — Art. 13(2) sentence 2 — Health-and-safety of users

**Source locus:**

> …with a view to minimising cybersecurity risks, preventing incidents and minimising their impact, **including in relation to the health and safety of users**.

**Instance 1 — POLY — S2 — `health and safety`**

`health` + `safety` — two dimensions.

- **R1:** Health only (physical/psychological harm).
- **R2:** Safety only (physical danger).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Health | In-Directive: literal |
| R2 | Safety | In-Directive: literal |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `and` |

R3 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §5.4.7 (AND).

**Instance 2 — SCOPE-Q — S2 — `users`**

`users` — natural persons only? Includes legal persons?

- **R1:** Natural persons (consumers + employees).
- **R2:** Legal persons (businesses).
- **R3:** Both.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Natural | In-Directive: Art. 3(18) `consumer` |
| R2 | Legal | None in OJ |
| R3 | Both | In-Directive: literal `users` (broader than `consumer`) |

R3 is the literal reading. S2.

*Berry anchor:* §3.3.1 (`users`).

**Layer 1 observation** — The `health and safety of users` clause is CRA-unique (no other regulation in the corpus has it). It bridges cybersecurity to physical safety. Layer 2 must adjudicate whether software-compliance analysis must include physical-safety risk assessment (probably no, but the boundary is contested).

| R4 | Any | In-Directive: literal `as appropriate` |

R4 is the literal reading. S2.

## CRA v0.2 — Annex I Essential Cybersecurity Requirements

> **Source.** OJ L, 20.11.2024, p. 68–69. `03_REFERENCE_MATERIAL/Regulatory_References/CRA.txt`, lines ~4153–4234.
>
> **Pattern.** Each Part quoted verbatim with highlighting, then per-clause analysis. Universal `[recital NN]` convention.
>
> **Annex I is the binding normative core of CRA.** Part I defines security properties for products; Part II defines vulnerability-handling obligations for manufacturers. These are referenced by Art. 6, 7, 8, 13, 27, 28, 32, and are the ultimate compliance target. 21 of the 26 T2 clauses lived in Annex I.

---

### 1. Annex I — full text

#### Part II — Vulnerability handling requirements

> Manufacturers of products with digital elements shall:
>
> **(1)** identify and document vulnerabilities and components contained in products with digital elements, including by drawing up a **software bill of materials** in a commonly used and machine-readable format covering at the very least the **top-level dependencies** of the products;
>
> **(2)** in relation to the risks posed to products with digital elements, **address and remediate vulnerabilities without delay**, including by providing security updates; where technically feasible, new security updates shall be provided **separately from functionality updates**;
>
> **(3)** apply **effective and regular tests and reviews** of the security of the product with digital elements;
>
> **(4)** once a security update has been made available, **share and publicly disclose** information about fixed vulnerabilities, including a description of the vulnerabilities, information allowing users to identify the product with digital elements affected, the impacts of the vulnerabilities, their severity and clear and accessible information helping users to remediate the vulnerabilities; in duly justified cases, where manufacturers consider the security risks of publication to outweigh the security benefits, they may **delay making public information** regarding a fixed vulnerability until after users have been given the possibility to apply the relevant patch;
>
> **(5)** put in place and enforce a **policy on coordinated vulnerability disclosure**;
>
> **(6)** take measures to **facilitate the sharing of information** about potential vulnerabilities in their product with digital elements as well as in third-party components contained in that product, including by providing a **contact address** for the reporting of the vulnerabilities discovered in the product with digital elements;
>
> **(7)** provide for mechanisms to **securely distribute updates** for products with digital elements to ensure that vulnerabilities are fixed or mitigated in a timely manner and, where applicable for security updates, in an automatic manner;
>
> **(8)** ensure that, where security updates are available to address identified security issues, they are **disseminated without delay** and, unless otherwise agreed between a manufacturer and a business user in relation to a tailor-made product with digital elements, **free of charge**, accompanied by advisory messages providing users with the relevant information, including on potential action to be taken.

---

---

### 3. Detailed cards

#### 3.1 CRA-CL129 — Part I (1) — Chapeau

**Source locus:**

> Products with digital elements shall be designed, developed and produced in such a way that they ensure an **appropriate level of cybersecurity based on the risks**.

**Instance 1 — VAG — S3 — `appropriate level of cybersecurity based on the risks`**

Same `appropriate` + `risks` double VAG as GDPR Art. 32(1) and NIS 2 Art. 21(1).

- **R1:** Quantitative risk threshold.
- **R2:** Qualitative risk judgment.
- **R3:** Sector-specific calibration.

| # | Reading | Disambiguation source |
|---|---|---|
| R1 | Quantitative | None in OJ |
| R2 | Qualitative | In-Directive: Art. 13(2) risk assessment |
| R3 | Sector-specific | None in OJ |

S3.

*Berry anchor:* §5.1 (`appropriate level`, `based on the risks`).

**Instance 2 — COORD — S2 — `designed, developed and produced`**

Same 3-phase coordination as Art. 13(1) CRA-CL14. S2.

**Layer 1 observation** — This is the CRA analog of NIS 2 Art. 21(1) opening and GDPR Art. 32(1) opening. The `appropriate level` qualifier propagates to all 13 sub-points of Part I (2)(a)–(m). Every sub-point inherits this VAG.
